Histeon works on the WordPress media library, adds collections to keep it tidy, compresses images before upload and writes by itself the attributes that make the right image load for each screen. Fonts, on the other hand, it serves directly from your site.

The media manager #

The Media manager is the window where you choose and upload images without leaving the builder. It opens in three ways:

  • from the Add menu (Ctrl+I) → Image from the library…, to insert a new image;
  • by selecting an image on the page and pressing Choose image… in the Properties panel, to replace it;
  • from the Media library button in the left bar (or from the command palette), to browse the whole library: images, video, audio and PDF.

Opened from the bar, the library inserts nothing on the first click: a click on a file shows its details on the right. Type, dimensions, size and upload date; the address, with Copy; the alternative text and the caption, with Save details; the collections; and Where it is used, that is the contents that name it (at any size) or have it as featured image. Contents you cannot edit are only counted. Insert in the page puts it after the selected element; Open in WordPress opens its page in wp-admin.

The images are those of the WordPress media library: what you upload here you find again in Media in wp-admin, and vice versa. To search, use the Search the library… field.

Collections #

Collections are folders for images, nested too: "Events", "Events → Concerts", "Icons"…

  • The left column shows All images, No collection and the tree of collections, each with its number of images.
  • To create a collection, type the name in the New collection… field. If you have one open, the field becomes Subcollection… and the new collection is created inside it.
  • A collection has no public pages: it only serves whoever builds the site.

To create collections and assign them, being able to upload files in WordPress is enough.

Uploading images #

  1. Open the collection you want to put the images in.
  2. Choose the Compression before upload (see the table).
  3. Press Upload and choose up to 20 files: each one has its own progress bar in the Uploads list.
CompressionWhat it does
Web (max 2560 px, AVIF or WebP)Reduces the longest side to 2560 px and converts to AVIF or WebP, keeping the lighter one. Good for almost everything.
Light (max 1600 px, AVIF or WebP)Like Web, with the longest side at 1600 px: for small images on the page or very lightweight sites.
OriginalThe file goes up as it is.

Compression happens in the browser, before sending: the server receives the already lightweight file. The orientation of photos taken with a phone is respected. JPEG, PNG and WebP are compressed. GIF, animated PNG and WebP, and SVG go up as they are, and so do files over 32 MB or 40 megapixels. A PNG never becomes AVIF: it stays lossless, or becomes a lossy WebP if that weighs less. The builder keeps the lightest format, and only if it weighs less than the original; otherwise the original is uploaded. A photo takes a few seconds. The encoders are Histeon Publish's, served by your own site: no file goes to outside services.

WordPress's limits always apply: allowed file types, maximum upload size and permission to upload files.

Your own presets and images already uploaded #

Presets…, next to the compression menu, adds the site's presets: a name, the longest side, the formats and the quality. The last preset used stays chosen.

An image already in the library is made lighter from its details: Compress with “preset” resizes and recompresses it in the same format, where it is. Its address does not change, the sizes WordPress made keep their names, and the original is set aside: Restore the original puts it back. When there is nothing to gain, the image stays as it was.

Alt text and details #

The ⋯ button on an image opens the Image details:

  • Alt text: the description for screen reader users and for search engines. Save alt text saves it on the image in the library, so it applies wherever you use it later.
  • The Collections the image belongs to.
  • Use this image to insert it into the page.

Video, audio and PDF #

From the Add menu → Video, audio or PDF from the library… the same manager opens with the File type filter. A video or an audio goes into the page with its playback controls; a PDF goes in as a link to the file.

Videos from YouTube or Vimeo, loaded on click #

From the Add menu → YouTube or Vimeo video… you paste the video's address. Histeon downloads the preview image once into the site's library and inserts a box with the preview and the play button. Visitors do not contact YouTube or Vimeo until they start the video: only then does the player load (from youtube-nocookie.com, or from Vimeo with “do not track”). The canvas shows the preview; try the player in the Preview.

Maps loaded on click #

From the Add menu → Map (OpenStreetMap or Google)… you paste an OpenStreetMap or Google Maps link (or the “embed” code they give) and type the place's name. The page shows a box with the name and “Load the map”: the map arrives, and the provider receives a request, only when a visitor opens it. Without JavaScript the link opens the map on the provider's site.

All the alternative texts of the page #

From the palette (Alternative texts of the page) or from Checks, a table shows every image of the page with its alternative text: write it right there, mark as Decorative the images that say nothing, and with Only without alternative text you see just the ones to fix.

Focal point #

When the box crops the image, in Properties click Focal point on the spot to keep in view (a face, a product); the arrows move it and Centre removes it.

A decorative image can have empty alt text; an image that says something (a product, a person, a chart) must describe it. The checks before publishing flag images without alt text.

Responsive images #

A phone screen doesn't need a 2560 px photo. WordPress creates several sizes of each uploaded image; Histeon makes sure the browser picks the right one.

Images chosen from the library #

When you insert an image from the media manager, Histeon writes in the element:

  • src, the original image;
  • srcset, the other sizes with the same proportions (cropped thumbnails are excluded, because they would distort the image);
  • sizes, the hint for the browser about how much space it takes up;
  • width and height, so the browser reserves the space and the page doesn't "jump" while loading;
  • alt, the alt text saved in the library.

They are normal attributes: you see and edit them in the Properties panel or in the HTML panel. If you change the layout, for example by putting the image in a narrow column, you can correct sizes by hand.

Images that come from content #

An image written with an expression, as in a post loop, gets the same attributes at visit time:

<img src={item.image.url} alt={item.image.alt}>

Histeon knows which library image it is, so it adds width, height, srcset and sizes. It adds only what is missing: what you wrote yourself is not touched.

  • For a specific WordPress size, write its name: {item.image.medium_large} uses that size, with its dimensions.
  • If the image has loading="lazy", sizes starts with auto: the browser uses the real width of the image on the page and downloads the most suitable file.
  • loading, decoding and fetchpriority are handled by WordPress, as for the rest of the site.

The image keys you can read are url, alt, width, height, caption, title, srcset, sizes and id.

Local fonts #

With Histeon Local fonts, the font files live on your site and are served by the site itself: pages don't request them from other servers, Google's included.

You find them in two places: in the builder, in Design system → Site fonts, and in Histeon → Settings, Local fonts section. Everyone who builds sees them; only administrators add or remove them.

From the Design system #

Site fonts shows the families served by the site, each with a sample written in its typeface and the weights it has. Copy copies the font stack ("Inter", sans-serif); with Histeon CSS the Text, Headings and Code buttons put it in the generator's typefaces, previewed in the canvas, and Save in development keeps it.

To add a family, type part of its name or its kind (serif, monospace…) in Add from Google Fonts: the list ships with Histeon, so searching doesn't ask Google anything. Choose the family, tick the weights (and Italic too, when there is one) and press Download to the site. If Google does not have one of the chosen weights, Histeon downloads the others. A family that is not in the list is added from Settings, by typing its name.

Importing from Google Fonts #

  1. In Import from Google Fonts enter the Family, for example Inter.
  2. In Weights enter the weights you need, separated by commas: 400, 700.
  3. Check italic too if you need it.
  4. Press Download to the site.

The server downloads the files from Google only once and from then on serves them from your site. Visitors to the pages never contact Google.

Uploading a file #

If you have your own font or one purchased with a web license:

  1. In Upload a file choose the file, in WOFF2 or WOFF format, at most 2 MB.
  2. Specify Family, Weight and Style (normal or italic).
  3. Press Upload. Repeat for each weight and style.

Histeon recognizes the file by its content, not by its name: a file that isn't really a font is rejected.

Using the fonts #

In CSS, you use them with the family name:

body {
  font-family: "Inter", system-ui, sans-serif;
}

The font rules reach the site's pages, the block editor and the builder canvas automatically, with text displayed immediately while the font loads. If you export the static site, the font files are copied together with the pages.

To remove a font, press Remove next to its family in the list.

Fonts are files and have no versions: adding or removing them takes effect immediately, without publishing a release. Before removing a font, check that the site's CSS no longer uses it.

Versions and releases

In Histeon you work on a copy of the site that only you can see, and you put it live when it's ready, all at once. If something goes wrong, you go back with one click. This chapter explains how it works, from saving to publishing, all the way to moving an entire site from one server to another.

Development and live #

Every page, template, component, stylesheet and setting in Histeon exists in two versions:

VersionWho sees itHow it changes
In developmentYou, the builder canvas and anyone with a preview linkEvery time you save in the builder
LiveSite visitorsOnly when you publish a release

Save (Ctrl+S) writes to development: the public site doesn't change. You can save as often as you like, close the builder, come back tomorrow. Published, in the top bar, opens the page as visitors see it right now.

Posts, the content of your own types and media have no Histeon versions: as in WordPress, their changes go live immediately. Versions cover the structure of pages, templates, components, styles, JavaScript and settings (queries, sources, filters, content types).

Text written by the client #

While you work in development, an editor can correct a text on the same page in the block editor. That change goes live immediately. Histeon doesn't lose it: when you load the document in the builder, and again when you publish, it merges the texts.

  • If you only changed the structure, the client's text stays.
  • If you both changed the same text, the builder's version wins and the publish window flags it as a conflict.

Publishing a release #

A release puts everything you chose live in one go. It has a version number (1.0.0, 1.0.1, 1.1.0…), notes and an author, and it is a complete snapshot of the site: that's why you can always go back to an earlier release.

From the builder #

  1. Press Publish… in the top bar.
  2. Under What goes live you find every document saved in development and not yet published. Uncheck the ones you don't want to publish now.
  3. Optional: Differences next to a document shows the lines that changed compared with the published site.
  4. Optional: the Checks before publishing and the Visual comparison (see below).
  5. Check the proposed Version (the next one, automatically) and write in the Notes what changes.
  6. Press Publish.

The approval of a reviewer, if there is one, also appears at the top of the window (see the chapter on interactions and code). Publishing is all or nothing: if one document can't be applied, none goes live.

From Histeon → Releases in wp-admin #

The Histeon → Releases screen shows the same thing outside the builder:

  • To publish, with the list of documents and the next release will be …. The version number can be left empty (Leave empty for the next one automatically.); the Notes are optional. Then Publish the selected documents.
  • Release history, with past releases, the one that is live, the restore entries, and the Restore and Export buttons.
  • The Static site, Import a release and Site move sections, described further on.

The Releases menu item shows a number when there are documents waiting.

To publish you need Histeon's publish permission. Administrators have it; you can give it to other roles with a role editor. Anyone who builds without that permission saves in development, and someone else publishes.

Scheduling a release #

Instead of publishing right away you can choose a day and a time: the release goes out by itself, but only if nothing changed since you scheduled it.

  1. In the builder press Publish… and choose the documents, the version and the notes as usual.
  2. Under When choose Schedule for… and enter the day and time. The time is always the site's (the time zone is shown next to the field, for example site time, Europe/Rome), even if you are in another country. At least 5 minutes ahead, at most 60 days.
  3. Press Schedule. With Block publishing if the checks find errors on, the checks must first end without errors, as for Publish.

From then on the Publish window and the Histeon → Releases screen show the line Scheduled for … at … by … · version … · N documents, with Cancel the schedule. Whoever scheduled it, or anyone who can publish, can cancel it; if someone else cancels it, whoever scheduled it gets an email. There is one scheduled release at a time; Publish now is always possible. The chosen version stays reserved: no other release takes it.

At the chosen time Histeon publishes with the permissions of whoever scheduled it, and only if:

  • that person still exists, can still publish and edit every chosen page;
  • every document is still as it was in development (the same save);
  • no other release touched those documents, and the version is still free;
  • no new contents arrived from Histeon CMS;
  • the client did not change, in Gutenberg, a text that the release changes too.

If anything does not match, nothing goes online: the release is skipped, the reason goes into the Log and whoever scheduled it gets an email. After the time the line becomes, for a few days, Version … published at … or Skipped the release scheduled for …: reason, until someone presses Close.

Good to know:

  • If you save a page of the scheduled release, that release will no longer go out. The builder says so with a bar above the page.
  • A release by hand (or a send from Histeon Publish) on the same documents cancels the scheduled one: the Publish window says so first. A release on other documents leaves it alone.
  • A restore cancels the scheduled release, after a confirmation that says so.
  • Deactivating Histeon cancels the scheduled release; reactivating it does not start it again.
  • The cron. WordPress runs scheduled things only when someone visits the site. If the site has no system cron (or is served as static files, with Static Bridge or on Cloudflare) the release can go out late; more than 2 hours late it does not go out and is skipped. The window and the Releases screen say so and show the line to add to the hosting's cron, plus the line define( 'DISABLE_WP_CRON', true ); for wp-config.php.
  • If you change the site's time zone after scheduling, the moment stays the same: the line shows it in the new time and recalls the time written then.
  • An AI agent cannot schedule releases, nor can the REST API with an application password: releases are scheduled only from the builder.
  • The same goes for publishing now, restoring a release and putting the static site online or rolling it back: with an application password the REST API refuses. A person does it from the builder or wp-admin; the signed links with Histeon Publish and staging stay as the administrator created them.

Seeing the page as another visitor #

In the builder's Preview, See as shows the page to someone not logged in, to a subscriber, to a shop customer (the site's roles that do not write). The conditions written with user change, for example {#if user.loggedIn} or {#if user.role == "customer"}. It is a test visitor: no account is used, and what other plugins show by role stays what a visitor without login sees.

Proposals for the client: A and B #

To let a client choose between two versions, in Histeon → Releases, section Proposals for the client:

  1. prepare the first version in the builder and save it in development;
  2. type the name (Proposal A), choose the page it opens on and press Save development as a proposal;
  3. change what you need, save, and save the second proposal (Proposal B).

Each proposal has its own preview link, valid for 7 days: it shows the live site with that proposal's changes, also to people without an account. Compare with opens a link with the two proposals side by side, to send to the client. Once they have decided, Choose this one brings the chosen proposal back into development (the other changes in development go back to what is live and stay in the history): check it and publish it like any release. Delete turns a proposal's link off.

Restoring #

You put an earlier release back live from Histeon → Releases (Restore in the history) or in the builder's Versions section.

  • Restoring creates a new release: history is never rewritten, and you can always go back to the release you started from, too.
  • Changes in development that haven't been published yet stay where they are.
  • If a release is scheduled, restoring cancels it (the confirmation says so).
  • In the builder, the option When restoring, keep the texts written after that version decides what happens to the texts: if it's on, the structure goes back to what it was then but the texts corrected in the meantime stay; if it's off, the texts from back then return as well.

A document's history #

Histeon → Versions lists the documents with their status (to publish or live). Open one and you see all its versions, newest first: every save in the builder, every change made in the block editor and every release.

  • Compare shows the differences between that version and the current one in development.
  • Restore brings that version back into development. It doesn't go live by itself: it will go with the next release.
  • The untrusted mark indicates a version written by someone without the unfiltered_html permission: it is sanitized again on every restore.

From the builder you get there from the Versions section with All versions of this page, with comparison and restore ↗.

How much the history weighs, and how to lighten it #

In Histeon → Overview, the Version history box says how many versions are saved, how much space they take in the database and since when. An administrator can choose Remove versions older than 6, 12 or 24 months and press Clean up the history: after a confirmation, the versions older than that date are gone and can no longer be restored. What is in development and live, the versions they start from and every release always stay, and every release can still be restored. The operation goes into the activity log.

Likewise Histeon forgets by itself, after 30 days, the reports of finished sends from Histeon Publish.

Checks before publishing #

The builder's Checks section looks at the page as it is drawn in the canvas and flags the most common problems. The icon shows the number of errors; Show selects the element and returns to the canvas.

AreaExamples of checks
AccessibilityImage without alternative text, link or button without text, field without a label, iframe without a title, skipped heading levels, insufficient text contrast, link with vague text (“click here”)
SEOPage without an H1, or with two H1s
PerformanceImage without width and height, image much larger than it appears

Each finding is an Error, To review or a Tip. Anything deliberately hidden from screen readers is not flagged.

In the Publish window, Check runs the development version of every page that changes (6 at most) through the same checks.

There it adds checks that make no sense in the canvas:

CheckSeverity
Page without a title (<title>)Error
Page without a language (lang)Error
WordPress asks search engines not to index the site (Settings → Reading, “Discourage search engines”): said only once, and it doesn't block publishingTo review
Page without a descriptionTo review
Page that asks not to be indexed (noindex)To review
Link to a page of the same site that doesn't exist (answer 404 or 410)To review
Page heavier than 1.5 MBTo review
Page already online without a canonical addressTip

Links are tried from your browser, without cookies, and only towards the same site: at most 200 per release, each for 5 seconds. A link to a page that exists only in development doesn't count as broken, and neither does a slow answer or one other than 404 and 410. Links to other sites aren't tried. The weight counts the HTML, style sheets, fonts and loaded images: scripts and images loaded further down are left out, so the real weight can be higher.

If the checks have finished when you press Publish, the release remembers what they found: Versions shows “Released with N errors and M warnings”. It is a reminder, not a proof, and it changes nothing about what can be done. A release published without finished checks, from Histeon → Releases, from a file or by Histeon CMS, has no such line.

Blocking publishing on errors #

In Histeon → Settings you can turn on Block publishing if the checks find errors. From then on:

  • the checks start by themselves when you open the Publish window, and the Publish button stays disabled as long as they find errors (warnings don't block);
  • Histeon → Releases no longer publishes and sends you to the builder.

The checks are an aid for whoever builds, not a full audit: they don't replace testing with a screen reader.

Visual comparison #

Also in the Publish window, Compare puts the Live page and the In development page side by side, on a large screen and on a phone, and marks the differences: in coral what is new or changes, in blue what disappears. Next difference jumps from one to the next.

It compares the open page and the pages or templates that change, 6 at most. Pages are shown without their scripts, so whatever a script draws after loading isn't part of the comparison. The comparison is optional and doesn't block publishing.

If you don't want to open the comparison page by page, the Appearance line in the page checks says how many pages change on a large screen and how many on a phone. It is worked out only when you press Calculate, because it loads every page four more times; View opens the visual comparison. It is information, not a warning.

Exporting and importing a release #

You can carry a release over to another Histeon site with an .xbrelease file: typically from the local or staging site to the production one.

Exporting. In Histeon → Releases, Export next to a release (or Export in the builder's Versions section) downloads the file. It contains the pages, components, templates, styles and settings of that release. Pages and posts are matched by slug, components by key, templates by name.

Importing.

  1. In Import a release choose the file and press Check the file.
  2. Histeon shows what would change, without writing anything yet.
  3. Import into development writes everything as changes in development. The source site's address becomes the current site's address.
  4. Check in preview, then publish a release as usual.

Missing pages and posts are created as drafts. Whatever can't be applied (a framework that isn't installed, an invalid query) ends up in the report.

File signatures #

The release and site-move files you export are signed with this site's key, and the preview of an import says who signed them:

  • Signed by this site, or by a site you trust: it imports as usual.
  • Signed by a key this site does not know: the preview shows the key's fingerprint (16 groups of 4 characters). Compare it with the one the source site shows in Histeon → Settings → Export signing, for example on the phone with whoever sent it. If it matches, tick I trust this key: from then on that site's files import without asking.
  • Not signed (an older file, or one from a site without the keys of wp-config.php): it imports only after ticking Import even without a signature, if you know where it comes from. In Settings, Require a signature always refuses them.
  • The signature does not match, or the key is revoked: the file is refused.

In Settings → Export signing you find this site's fingerprint, Generate a new key (sites that trust the old one move to the new one by themselves) and the list of sites you trust, each with Revoke.

Exporting and importing releases is reserved for administrators. Imported content has the trust level of whoever imports it: without unfiltered_html, the markup is sanitized and the JavaScript isn't imported.

From staging to production #

If you work on a staging site, that is a test copy, you can send what you published to production, images included, without files to download and upload.

  1. On the production site, in Histeon → Releases → Receive from a staging site, choose whether the staging site may also publish and press Create the pairing code. Copy the code: it is shown only once.
  2. On the staging site, in Histeon → Releases → Send to production, paste the code and press Link.
  3. Choose What the latest release changed or The whole site, then press Preview on production: you see what would change there, without anything being written.
  4. Press Send to production. If the code doesn't allow publishing, everything arrives in development on production and is published from there with a release.

The code contains the link's secret: don't send it by plain email. Every request is signed and works only once. On production you can revoke the link at any time, and the Log of both sites records every sending.

Site move #

A release carries only Histeon documents. The Site move, in Histeon → Releases, carries the whole site in an .xbsite file: pages, posts, components, templates, menus, styles, settings, the content of your own types and the media they use.

Not included: users, comments, other plugins' options, revisions and version history.

Exporting. Export the whole site downloads the file with what is live; changes in development are not included.

Importing.

  1. Upload the Site file (.xbsite) and press Check the file: you see what would be created, updated or skipped.
  2. Choose:
    • Import into development: media uploaded, documents as changes in development, new content as drafts. Nothing goes live until you publish;
    • Import and publish: puts everything live, with the status each piece of content had on the source site.
  3. Cancel deletes the uploaded file. A pending file stays available for 30 minutes.

Importing the same file twice doesn't duplicate anything: media and content are found again. This is useful when the file contains new content types: the first import creates them, the second imports their content.

Only administrators can move a site. Very large files depend on the server's upload limit. Content deleted on the source site is not deleted on the destination site.

The Histeon CMS package as a file #

The Histeon CMS package is the one Histeon Publish uses: pages, posts with categories and tags, components, templates with their role (home, single post, archives, 404, search…), styles, contents and media, all in one .xbsite file. It is not the site move: it holds the development copies and can be sent back with the changes.

In Histeon → Releases → Histeon CMS → The package as a file:

  • Download the package saves it to your computer, to open in Histeon Publish even without a link;
  • Package to import + Check verifies a package exported by Histeon Publish and shows what it would create or update, without writing anything. Then Import into development (it goes live with the next release) or Import and publish. Cancel deletes the file; a pending file stays available for 30 minutes.

A template with a role becomes WordPress's template for that case (for example single for posts): if the theme already has a Site Editor customization with that name, the import stops and does not overwrite it. Only posts made with Histeon travel; those written with the block editor stay out.

Only administrators download and upload the package. A package of another project is refused: the site stays tied to a single Histeon CMS project.

What stops a send from Histeon Publish #

Before it receives a send the site reads its own content back. Content that cannot travel (for example an image from another site or an expression with url.query) stays out and is named, but if Histeon Publish already knows it the send stops with its name: left out, it would look deleted. Fix it on the site, then try again.

  • url.path travels (Histeon 0.5.4): a menu that marks the active item with {#if url.path == "/festival/"} is fine. Histeon Publish does not send a project that uses it to an older site, and says so.
  • A library image written with the other scheme (http:// on a site moved to https) is the same image and travels.
  • Every template has its page content block, archive templates too: on an archive it stays empty, on the site and in Histeon Publish. The builder warns when a template lacks it (chapter 9).

The home page after the first send #

On a new site WordPress opens / on the list of posts, and the project's home is at /home/. After the first send with publishing, Histeon Publish asks whether the site should open on the project's home and, if the project has a “blog” page, list the posts there. Only a yes changes the site's Settings → Reading (home page and posts page), live at once; it needs a link with the publish permission. The Log records it as cms_front_page. The addresses of posts do not change. It can always be changed again in Settings → Reading.

Converting an HTML page: the report #

In Histeon → Releases, Convert an HTML page turns a page, a site's ZIP or its folder into a Histeon project. The conversion's report is split by what happened:

  • Removed: what does not go into a Histeon site (scripts, iframes, event handlers, files too large, styles that cannot be carried over);
  • Moved into the page's JavaScript: event handlers and <dialog> windows that work again through the page's JavaScript;
  • Media queries and breakpoints: the widths of the site's media queries, how often, and whether they match a named media query (--mobile, --tablet, --desktop) or which is nearest;
  • To check: the rest, for example images without alternative text or forms without an address to send to.

Histeon Publish shows the same report when it converts a site.

A Histeon prototype in a single file (the HTML with data-histos-prototype="1", as an AI agent writes it) is not converted: it is imported as it is, with templates, components, contents and loops, into the site's Histeon project. Images written as data: come with the file; those given by an https address are downloaded by the browser when the site hosting them allows it, otherwise the import stops and says which one to upload to WordPress.

Importing a page of this site #

In the same panel, Import a page of this site copies into Histeon a page the site already has, made with another tool (for example with WordPress blocks). A new Histeon page is created, in development; the original page stays as it is.

What to expect:

  • the look is copied: the structure is left to refine in the builder, with the original tool's classes;
  • only the page's content comes in: the theme's header, menu, footer and admin bar stay out, with their scripts (below);
  • parts and components are not created: repeated cards stay inside the page;
  • fonts and files of other sites do not come in (for example a font service): the site's font is shown;
  • Histeon CSS fonts and sizes stay the site's; the colours are your choice (below).

How to do it:

  1. press Import a page of this site: the list of the published pages you can edit appears, with a box to search them. Pages already made with Histeon and password-protected pages are not there, and the panel says how many;
  2. choose the page and, in Colours of the page, which colours you want (below); then press Import this page. The browser downloads it as a visitor sees it, without your cookies, with the style sheets, images and fonts of this site: never from other sites, never from the administration or the API. Nothing of the page is run. The site's own Histeon CSS and the admin bar stay out;
  3. the new page takes the first free address after the original one (/about-2/, then -3…);
  4. the report says what comes in, what the site already had the same, what it already had different (the site's styles win, the page's do not come in) and what stays out;
  5. Go on to the check and confirm: as for any package, nothing is written before.

One page is imported at a time, and more can be imported later: the styles the site already has do not change. If someone changes the site's styles between the reading and the confirmation, the import stops with a conflict: convert again.

The page's styles apply only to that page. Its rules, even those written on body (background, text colour), do not touch the other pages of the site: its main elements carry a mark (data-histos-pagina="about-2") and every rule of the page applies only where the mark is. An animation or a font the site already had with the same name comes in with a new name, and the report says so. Limits to know:

  • a new element you add in the builder at the top level of the page has no mark and does not take the page's styles: put it inside a section that is already there;
  • the site's header and footer, on that page, no longer take the page's rules;
  • of the original page only the content comes in: its <main> (which becomes a <div>, because the site has its own), with a block theme without <main> what stands between the theme's header and footer, with a classic theme #main, #content or .site-main. The theme's header, menu, footer, sidebar and admin bar stay out, with their scripts and sheets; the report says where the content was taken from. If the content is not recognised, the page comes in whole and a warning says so: remove the copied header and footer in the builder.

The page's colours. The conversion writes the page's two brand colours as Histeon CSS variables, chosen only among the page's own sheets and styles: those of the theme, of WordPress (the --wp--preset--* palette) and of the plugins do not count. In the site a variable has the site's value. Before importing, choose:

  • The page keeps its colours (checked on opening): the site does not change and the page has the original's colours;
  • The page takes the site's colours: the site does not change, and the page's brand colours become the site's;
  • The site's colours become the page's: this changes the look of the whole site, in its colours only. It is offered only when the site uses Histeon CSS (Histeon's framework with the Histeon CSS module on); otherwise a line says why it is missing. Before going on, the list of the variables that change appears, "variable: before → after", for the light and dark themes, with a sample of each colour: press Yes, change the site's colours to go on, or Cancel to go back to the choice. Fonts, sizes and the rest of the design do not change; the pages change in development, and online with the release.

The page's other colours are values written in the page and stay its own with every choice. The report says which of the page's colours became variables and what was kept.

The site must already have a Histeon project: if it has none, the first page is converted with Convert and becomes the project's home.

Overview and site status #

Histeon → Overview is the page to start from. It shows:

  • whether everything is live or what is To publish, with Review and publish;
  • the live release and since when, the number of pages, posts and components;
  • Recently modified, with Open in Histeon;
  • Latest releases. The first publish creates version 1.0.0.

The Site status card gathers what deserves a look, with a green dot (fine) or a coral one (needs a look) and the link to fix it: the status of the modules (on, off, active as a separate plugin, stopped after an error), the static copy (static pages and pages to regenerate), the recommended imports. When there's nothing to report it says all good.

SEO and performance

A beautiful site also has to be found and load quickly. Histeon works with the SEO plugins you already know and sends visitors only what the page really uses: CSS, scripts, images at the right size.

SEO with your favorite plugin #

Histeon doesn't replace your SEO plugin: titles, descriptions, social data and sitemaps stay with Yoast SEO, Rank Math, Slim SEO or The SEO Framework. Histeon makes sure these plugins see pages the way a visitor sees them.

Why it's needed #

Histeon saves text in a structured way, inside blocks. An SEO plugin reading the "raw" content of a Histeon page would find zero words, no headings and no images. That's why Histeon hands it the page already rendered, with real text, headings and images.

What you get #

WhereWhat happens
Yoast SEO and Rank Math analysis in the block editorThe plugin analyzes the page's real text, with headings and images, and updates shortly after every change
Yoast SEO and Rank Math sitemapsImages on Histeon pages, including those that come from content, go into the sitemap
Slim SEOReads Histeon pages for the description and title, and adds their images to the sitemap
The SEO FrameworkUses the page text for generated descriptions and its images for social, after the featured image
WordPress's automatic excerptIf a page has no written excerpt, it takes the first words of the page. It is used by variables such as Yoast's %%excerpt%%, Rank Math's %excerpt% and by feeds

What you write by hand always wins: an excerpt or meta description written by the author takes precedence over text taken from the page.

What SEO plugins see #

The page Histeon hands to SEO plugins is the live one, rendered as an anonymous visitor sees it:

  • changes in development don't make it into descriptions and sitemaps until you publish them;
  • nothing personal (the logged-in user's name, restricted content) can end up in a public description;
  • password-protected pages produce nothing.

The result is cached and rebuilt when the page changes, when you publish a release or when you update Histeon.

In the block editor, on the other hand, the analysis uses the page with the permissions of whoever is editing it: you see the effect of what you write right away.

The builder's SEO checks #

The builder's Checks section also flags the most common SEO problems: a page without an H1 heading, or with two H1s. Components and template parts don't need an H1. See the chapter on versions and releases.

Development preview links explicitly ask search engines not to index them.

CSS on demand #

Many sites send every page hundreds of KB of CSS, almost all of it useless for that page. Histeon reads the final HTML of each page and includes only the rules for the classes and IDs that are really there.

  • Histeon styles reach the page only if an element uses them.
  • The CSS framework (Histeon CSS, Core Framework, Automatic.css): Histeon can do the same with the framework's rules too. You choose this in Histeon → Settings, CSS loading section:
    • Framework on demand (recommended): each page receives only the framework rules it uses. Variables and general rules always stay.
    • Full framework: the whole stylesheet on every page, as the framework loads it.
  • When in doubt, a rule is kept: one extra byte is better than a broken page.

The CSS arrives inside the page itself, with no separate files to download. With large global sheets, Global sheets in a cached file in the same section is worth it: those sheets come in a file the browser reuses from page to page.

Classes added by scripts #

Histeon only sees the classes present in the page's HTML. If a script adds a class after loading (a menu that opens, a dialog that appears, results loaded later), its rules might be missing.

In that case, write those classes in Classes and IDs always included (Histeon → Settings, CSS loading section), separated by spaces. A trailing * works as a prefix: is-* includes all classes that start with is-.

If the plugin of a framework other than the one you chose is also active, that plugin may load its own stylesheet on every page, outside Histeon's on-demand loading: the settings point this out.

Scripts only where needed #

Histeon doesn't load scripts "just in case" on every page:

  • the interactions script (tabs, mobile menu, reveal on scroll) weighs a few KB and arrives only on pages that use one;
  • the loop filters script arrives only on pages that use filters;
  • the page JavaScript and the components' JavaScript arrive only where needed, once, after the content.

Without JavaScript, pages remain usable: interactions are shown open and filters work by reloading the page.

Images that load well #

Images are almost always the heaviest part of a page. Histeon helps in three places.

  • On upload. The media manager resizes images and converts them to AVIF or WebP in the browser, before sending them (see the chapter on media and fonts).
  • In the page. An image picked from the library gets srcset, sizes, width and height. An image that comes from content (<img src={item.image.url}>) gets the same attributes at visit time, without touching the ones you wrote yourself.
  • No jumps. width and height make the browser reserve the image's space before it arrives: the page doesn't shift while it loads.

If an image has loading="lazy", Histeon adds auto to sizes, so the browser picks the file based on the image's real space. loading, decoding and fetchpriority are handled by WordPress.

The builder's Checks flag images without width and height and those much larger than they appear.

Fonts without external requests #

Local fonts live on your site: no requests to Google or other servers when someone visits the pages. It's faster (no extra connection) and more respectful of privacy. Text shows up right away in a system font and switches to the chosen font as soon as it has arrived. See the chapter on media and fonts.

Checklist for a fast page #

  1. Leave Framework on demand on.
  2. Upload images with Web or Light compression and insert them from the media manager.
  3. Use local fonts instead of remote ones.
  4. Prefer Histeon interactions over heavy external libraries.
  5. Before publishing, look at the Checks section and fix the errors.
  6. If the site allows it, serve it as a static site (see the chapter on the static site).

Static site

A static site is made of plain HTML files: no PHP or database on every visit. It's faster, it copes better with traffic spikes and it offers fewer points of attack. With Histeon you keep working in WordPress as always, and visitors receive pages that are already built.

There are three routes, which you can also combine:

RouteWhere the pages liveWhen to choose it
Static zipWherever you like: you download it and upload it yourselfFor an archive, a handover, file-only hosting
Static BridgeOn the same server as WordPressThe site stays on its hosting, but visitors receive static HTML
CloudflareOn Cloudflare Workers, in your accountThe public site lives on Cloudflare, with versions and rollback to an earlier version

What goes into the static copy #

Each page is generated as an anonymous visitor sees it: no admin bar, no data about the logged-in user, no changes in development. Included are:

  • the home page, public published content (without a password), the archives of public content types and taxonomies;
  • the assets the pages use (CSS, scripts, images, fonts), copied directly from the server's disk;
  • a 404.html page.

The site's links become relative, so the copy works on any domain.

The security check #

Before creating a zip or publishing to Cloudflare, Histeon checks all the generated pages. It stops if it finds something that must never leave the site:

  • server paths;
  • development preview links;
  • internal builder annotations;
  • WordPress session codes (the "nonces"), which expire and would break the page.

Instead, it flags with a warning, without stopping, links to the WordPress admin or API and the administrator's email.

Histeon blocks the static copy if a page contains a session code, which can't end up in files that are the same for every visitor. Histeon doesn't make a static copy of a FluentCart store: the store stays dynamic, or it has to be served with Static Bridge, excluding the store pages.

The static zip #

  1. Open Histeon → Releases, Static site section.
  2. Press Download the static site (zip).
  3. Histeon generates each page, checks it and, if all is well, downloads the zip.

On large sites it can take a few minutes. If the check finds blocking problems, the zip is not created and a message explains why. The zip contains only the pages and their assets: no PHP, no hidden files. No copy stays on the server: the temporary files are deleted after the download.

Only administrators can download the static site.

Static Bridge #

Static Bridge is an open source (GPL) plugin that serves pages as static HTML from the same server as WordPress. The admin, the login, requests with parameters and the paths you declare as dynamic keep going to WordPress.

It's included in Histeon as a module: you turn it on in Histeon → Modules. It requires PHP 8.4. If you already installed it as a separate plugin, Histeon recognizes it and uses that one, with the same data. Once it's on, its pages (Generate, Settings, Diagnostics, Activity…) appear at the bottom of the Histeon menu.

First run #

  1. Turn on the module and open the Static Bridge settings: confirm the domain and check the dynamic paths.
  2. Configure the web server following the Static Bridge documentation: with Nginx, manual work is needed.
  3. Run the Diagnostics and fix the errors.
  4. From Generate, start the first full generation.
  5. Run the Diagnostics again and try a few public pages, the login and the admin.

Do the first trial run on a test site with a backup, with fallback to WordPress on and "strict" mode off, as the Static Bridge documentation recommends.

What Histeon does for Static Bridge #

On every release, Histeon tells Static Bridge which pages to regenerate:

  • a modified component updates only the pages that use it;
  • styles and settings update the whole site;
  • a modified post also updates the pages with a loop, data. data or loop filters that might show it.

After publishing in the builder, the message says that Static Bridge will shortly update the static copy of the pages involved. In Histeon → Releases, the Static Bridge card shows whether the copy is being served, how many static pages there are, whether it updates by itself on every release, the pages in the queue and the latest activity, with Open Static Bridge. The same status appears in Histeon → Overview, in the Site status card.

Publishing to Cloudflare #

Histeon publishes the complete static site (pages and assets) to Cloudflare Workers in your account. Every publish is a version: you can go back to an earlier one without regenerating anything.

Setup #

  1. In Cloudflare, create an API token with only the Account › Workers Scripts › Edit permission.
  2. In Histeon → Settings, Static site section → Publishing to Cloudflare, enter the Account ID, Worker name and API token.
  3. If you like, check Automatic: Publish to Cloudflare on every release, in the background.
  4. Press Save Cloudflare settings.

Even better: ask whoever manages the server to write the token in wp-config.php (constant XB_CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN), so it isn't stored in the database. If you save it in the settings, it is encrypted and never shown again.

Publishing and rolling back #

In the builder, the Versions section has a Cloudflare part:

  • Publish to Cloudflare now generates the static site of what is published and uploads it. Only changed files are sent.
  • The list shows the latest publishes, with their status (live, blocked if the security check found problems).
  • Put live again brings back an earlier version immediately.

With the automatic option, every Histeon release goes to Cloudflare by itself, in the background, without slowing down publishing. Publishing and rolling back require the publish permission.

Cloudflare limits the number of files per version and the size of each file (20,000 files and 25 MB when this manual was written): the current limits, paid plans included, are in Cloudflare's documentation.

What stays dynamic #

A static site can't respond to each visitor differently. Here's what happens to the dynamic parts.

FeatureStatic BridgeStatic zipCloudflare
Loop filtersThe page without filters is static; picking a filter changes the address and the request goes to WordPressOnly the first page of results remainsOnly the first page of results remains
Histeon forms (/xb-form)——They work: the Worker receives them and forwards them to WordPress
Other plugins' formsThey go to WordPress; Static Bridge can handle Fluent FormsNot handled by Histeon: check case by caseNot handled by Histeon: check case by case
Cart and checkoutThey stay dynamic in the store“Add to cart” links lead to the site“Add to cart” links lead to the site
Logged-in usersThey get WordPress——

Other things to know:

  • Pages that read address parameters (url.query) or loop filters depend on the request: with Static Bridge they always go to WordPress when the address has parameters.
  • Data that changes on its own (for example an external service read with data.) reaches the static copy at the next regeneration.
  • Development previews never end up in the static copy.

Forms on the Cloudflare site #

The site published on Cloudflare has only one dynamic address, /xb-form. The Worker verifies the Turnstile anti-bot check, forwards the fields to WordPress with a signature, and the message appears in Form messages in wp-admin, with an email notification to the administrator.

To turn it on, in Histeon → Settings, Static site forms (Turnstile) section, enter the Turnstile secret key and press Send to the Worker: the key goes to Cloudflare and doesn't stay on the site. How to build the form is explained in the chapter on interactions and code.

Static Bridge and Cloudflare together #

The two kinds of publishing don't exclude each other: both react to the same Histeon release. Static Bridge keeps the site fast on its hosting; Cloudflare publishes a self-contained copy, for example as the main site or as a fallback.

Multilingual sites

Histeon builds sites in several languages together with Lingua Bridge, the free (GPL) multilingual plugin designed to work with Histeon. This chapter explains how to turn it on, how to translate pages, components and site texts, and how to put a new language live without surprises.

How it works #

Each language is a regular WordPress page, linked to the original by Lingua Bridge. The English version of the “About us” page is a piece of content in its own right, which opens in the builder like any other page.

A few useful things follow from this:

  • Everything you know applies to every language: saving in development, releases, versions, review and preview work the same way on every translation.
  • Each language can be different: one more image, one less section, a text rewritten for a different audience.
  • A structural change doesn't spread by itself: if you add a section to the original, it has to be added in the translations (Lingua Bridge tells you what changed, see below).
  • Styles, settings and queries are the same for all languages: you change them only once.
  • Deactivating Lingua Bridge leaves the pages as regular WordPress content, and they remain editable.

Histeon doesn't depend on any particular language plugin: it offers hooks that Lingua Bridge uses. Lingua Bridge is, however, the only engine that is tested and recommended.

Turning on Lingua Bridge #

Lingua Bridge is included in Histeon as a module, off by default.

  1. Open Histeon → Modules.
  2. Turn on Lingua Bridge with its switch.
  3. The Lingua Bridge menu appears under Histeon. Open its Settings.
  4. In the Languages tab, choose the default language (the language of the original) and the translation languages.

If Lingua Bridge is already installed as a separate plugin, the module stays idle and the plugin keeps working as before: the data is the same. You'll find the details in the chapter Modules and updates.

Warning: after the first linked translation, the default language can no longer be changed and languages already in use can't be removed. Choose them carefully.

The Lingua Bridge dashboard has a Getting started list that guides you through the first steps. The translations to do (to translate, needing an update, in draft) also appear in Histeon → Overview, in the site status.

Language addresses #

In the URLs and SEO tab of the Lingua Bridge settings you'll find Language addresses: “Each language in its own folder: /en/, /en/about-us/…”. This is the recommended setting:

  • the original stays at the site root (/chi-siamo/), translations in their language's folder (/en/about-us/);
  • search engines, the language switcher and the static copy always know which language they're looking at;
  • Lingua Bridge adds hreflang to the linked pages and the language sitemap;
  • lists, searches and loops show the content in the page's language, in the builder canvas too.

It requires pretty permalinks (WordPress's Settings → Permalinks). The feature stays off until you turn it on.

If there's still another multilingual plugin on the site (Polylang, WPML, TranslatePress), first complete the import from Import from plugin and check that the two aren't competing for the same addresses. Don't use two language plugins on the same paths.

With the static site, each language is a folder of the static copy (en/…), with no redirects based on the browser.

Translating a page in Histeon #

When the page has languages, the builder's top bar shows the language bar, next to the title:

What you seeWhat it means
The highlighted code (for example IT)The language of the page you're editing
A plain code (EN)A translation that already exists: one click opens it in the builder
A code with a plus sign (+DE)A missing language: one click creates the translation and opens it in Histeon

The translation created with +DE starts as a draft, a copy of the original, and Lingua Bridge tells you which fields it copied and which are still to be translated. Then you translate by typing directly in the canvas (double-click on a text) or in the HTML panel, as on any page.

While you work on a translation, the builder's checks flag texts of at least 12 characters that are still identical to the original, with the message “Same as the original, maybe not translated”. A proper name or a brand can stay the same on purpose: in that case ignore the warning.

In a component's sessions there is no language bar: components are translated as content in their own right (below).

Translating components #

Header, footer and the other components are WordPress content too, and they're translated the same way: you create the component's translation and translate it.

On the English page, Histeon uses the English version of the component only if it's published. While it's a draft, or if it doesn't exist, the original is shown. That way a half-done translation never ends up on the site by mistake.

Translating on the site #

Lingua Bridge also has a visual editor on the site: you translate on the real page, with the real theme.

  1. On a page of the site, from the admin bar choose Translate this page and the language.
  2. If the translation is missing, it's created as a draft and opens right away.
  3. Hover over the texts: those to translate are highlighted in yellow. Click a text (or an image, for its alternative text) and write the translation in the panel.
  4. Ctrl+Enter saves and moves to the next text. Next to translate takes you to the next text still to be translated.
  5. Leave as is marks as done a text that stays the same by choice.
  6. Publish the translation puts it live, if you have permission.

For each text, the panel shows the sentence from the original and the glossary terms it contains.

Strings: texts outside the pages #

A page also shows texts that aren't part of its content: the site title and tagline, menu labels, theme and plugin texts. You translate them in Lingua Bridge → Strings.

  • Title, tagline and menu labels appear by themselves the first time you open the screen.
  • Other texts are added by writing them exactly as they are in the original language, or with one click in the editor on the site.
  • Only whole texts are replaced, and only as plain text: a string can't add code to a page.
  • Strings are translated by whoever administers the site.

Themes and plugins with a WordPress translation pack translate themselves: Lingua Bridge uses the right WordPress language for each language of the site, and can install missing packs from the Languages tab.

Machine translation and memory #

Machine translation. In Settings → AI translation you can connect a translation service with your own key: DeepL, or an OpenAI-compatible service (including a model on your own server). Save and test translates a test sentence. Then:

  • in the editor on the site, Machine translate translates the sentences still in the original language and leaves the ones already translated alone;
  • in the dashboard, the bulk action Machine translate (drafts) creates the missing drafts and translates them.

Machine translation works only on drafts: a published translation is never touched. The key is never shown.

Translation memory. Lingua Bridge remembers sentences already translated and suggests them again: a new translation starts with the known sentences already in place, and in the editor on the site they appear as “From memory”, with Use or Apply the memory. The memory learns from a machine translation only when someone publishes it, that is, after reviewing it.

When the original changes. The translation becomes “needs update” and the editor on the site shows only the new or changed sentences. On a draft, Update the draft rebuilds it, keeping the sentences already translated; on a published translation you correct the sentences by hand and then choose Mark as updated.

Translating with an AI agent #

With the Histeon connector connected (chapter Histeon and artificial intelligence), the ✦ panel shows Translate this page (Lingua Bridge drafts): choose the languages and press Translate. The agent goes through the Lingua Bridge connector and leaves drafts only. For a language that's already published it creates a correction draft, without touching the live page.

Lingua Bridge also has a guided path of its own, in Lingua Bridge → Translate with AI.

XLIFF for translators and agencies #

In Lingua Bridge → XLIFF (agencies) you export the content to translate to an XLIFF file, the format of professional translation tools (Trados, memoQ, Phrase, Smartcat, OmegaT…).

  1. Choose the language, the content type and what to export (to translate, needing an update, or everything for a review).
  2. Press Export XLIFF and send the file to the translator. Each text is a segment; code doesn't go into it.
  3. When the translated file comes back, upload it on the same screen.

Each piece of content becomes a draft with the original's structure and the translated texts; a translation that's already published gets a correction draft. Nothing is published.

If the original changed after the export, the file won't import: export again, and the translation tool will find the sentences already done in its memory.

The language switcher #

The simplest way, in Histeon, is the Language switcher snippet: open Add an element (Ctrl+I), Interactions group, and put it in the header (usually in its component).

  • It appears only when the page has at least two published languages.
  • It leads to the published versions of the page, with hreflang, lang and the current language marked for screen readers.
  • Its classes follow the lang-switch BEM: style it like any other element.

Alternatively, Lingua Bridge can add it by itself at the end of the menu: Settings → URLs and SEO → Language switcher in the menu.

Before putting a language live #

A new language is visible on the site as soon as its pages are published. It's good practice not to switch on a language on a site that's already live without having checked it:

  1. Prepare the translations as drafts, including those of the components (header and footer) and of the strings.
  2. Check every page in preview, with the builder's checks showing no unexpected “maybe not translated” warnings.
  3. If you can, try the per-language addresses on a copy of the site first: turning them on changes the translations' URLs, and with a static site or a CDN the outputs need to be regenerated and redirects from the old addresses prepared.
  4. Publish the translations together, then add the language switcher.
  5. With the static site, after changing the strings regenerate the complete static copy: a string applies to all pages.

Histeon and artificial intelligence

Histeon has no built-in assistant and, on its own, doesn't send your data to AI services. Instead, it works with the agent you already use on your computer (Claude Code, Codex, Opencode or any agent with a shell or an MCP client), and gives it clear rules: what it can do, with which permissions, and above all what always stays in your hands.

Data leaves only when you send it: when you connect an agent, what it reads from the site (pages, styles, components, framework, the site brief) reaches the agent and the AI service the agent uses; when you enter a key of your own, for example for Lingua Bridge's machine translation, the texts to translate go to the service you chose.

There are two routes:

RouteWhen to use itWhere the agent works
Histeon connectorYou work in the builder and want to watch the agent at workIn the builder tab you have open, with your changes visible as they happen
WordPress abilitiesA remote agent, with no tab openThrough an MCP server on the site, as a WordPress user

In both cases the same rule applies: the agent's changes go to development. The live site only changes when someone publishes a release.

The Histeon connector #

The connector is a small program the agent starts on your computer. It acts as a bridge between the agent and the builder tab.

  1. In the builder, click ✦ in the left bar. The AI connector panel opens.
  2. Press Copy the instructions and paste them into your agent's chat. The instructions tell it to:
    • download histos-connector.mjs from this site (there's also the Download histos-connector.mjs link);
    • start it with the exact command, which contains your pairing secret;
    • read the manual for AIs (manual) and the site summary (brief) before making changes;
    • wait for the requests you write in the builder.
  3. Press Connect this tab. Until the connector responds, ✦ pulses and the status says “Waiting for the connector…”; then it turns the accent color and the status says “Connected”.
  4. Ask the agent for what you need, in its chat or from Ask the agent (below).
  5. Disconnect closes the connection. Closing the tab closes it too.

You need Node 18 or later on your computer, nothing else to install. The builder must be open over HTTPS, or on localhost.

While the agent works:

  • you see every change in the canvas, right away;
  • every operation can be undone with Undo, like yours;
  • the ✦ panel lists the Agent operations, with any errors;
  • saving goes to development, and only when you agree.

The ✦ panel also offers Manual for the AI: it's the manual the agent reads in order to work the way someone who knows Histeon would (BEM classes, framework variables, components, accessibility). You can read it too.

The pairing secret #

The command to paste contains a secret of yours: with it, the connector and your tab recognize each other. That way no website and no other program on the computer can connect in their place.

  • Don't share the copied instructions: they contain the secret.
  • If you shared it by mistake, open Pairing in the ✦ panel and choose Regenerate the secret. Then copy the instructions again and restart the connector.

The connector listens only on your computer (127.0.0.1), and the agent's commands require a token that stays on your computer.

Ask the agent #

In the ✦ panel, the Ask the agent section lets you write to the agent without going through its chat.

Create a section. Describe the section in the Describe the section to create field (for example “three testimonials with photo, name and role, on a light background”) and press Create a section. The section goes after the selected element, or at the end of the page if nothing is selected. With Histeon CSS active, the agent uses its tokens and BEM classes written for the section.

Translate this page. Appears when the page has languages (Lingua Bridge). Choose the languages and press Translate: the agent creates only translation drafts; for a language that's already published, a correction draft. See Multilingual sites.

Requests are queued:

StatusMeaning
waiting for the agentThe agent hasn't picked it up yet. You can withdraw it with ✕
the agent is working on itThe agent is doing it
doneFinished: the agent's note appears below, with what it did and what's left to decide

Requests live in the tab: reloading the page loses them. At most 20 open requests, of 2000 characters each.

Working from files #

If you prefer a code editor (VS Code or another), with the tab connected the connector can bring the page into a folder, synced both ways: Work from files (VS Code) in the ✦ panel shows the command.

  • One subfolder per document, with page.html and a .css file for each selector.
  • You save in your editor and the builder updates; you change something in the builder and the file follows.
  • If both change, neither overwrites the other: the builder's version goes next to the file, with the .builder extension.
  • Scripts aren't synced, and saving remains a builder action.

The connector as an MCP server #

If your agent uses MCP, you can register the connector once and for all. The exact commands, with your pairing, are in As an MCP server in the ✦ panel. For example, with Claude Code:

claude mcp add xb -- node /percorso/histos-connector.mjs mcp --origin <sito> --pair <segreto>

The builder tab still has to be open and connected: the agent always works there.

WordPress abilities #

Since WordPress 6.9, plugins can offer abilities (Abilities API), that is, actions a remote agent calls through a WordPress MCP adapter. Histeon offers these:

AbilityWhat it does
List of documentsThe content the user can edit with Histeon
Read a documentThe structure of the development version
Render a documentThe HTML of the development version
FrameworkThe framework's tokens, variables and classes
Unpublished changesDocuments with changes in development
Preview linkA signed link to the preview
Apply operationsStructured changes (text, attributes, insert, move, remove), in development
Set a styleThe CSS of a class selector, in development
Ask to publish a releaseLists the development changes and leads to the Releases page, where a person publishes (see below)

The agent authenticates as a WordPress user, for example with an application password, and has exactly that user's permissions, no more.

At most 100 operations per call: a single error and nothing is saved. If the agent states the version it read and the document has changed in the meantime (for example because you're working on it), the call is rejected instead of overwriting.

Publishing with abilities. An agent doesn't publish on its own. The Ask to publish a release ability, which needs the publish permission (xb_publish), lists what would go live and returns the Releases page: there a person checks and publishes. It's still best to give the agent a user that does not have the publish permission.

What an agent can and cannot do #

It canIt cannot
Read the page, styles, components and the frameworkRun code in the builder or on the server: it only picks from a closed list of operations
Change structure, texts and styles, in developmentPublish from the connector: the operation doesn't exist
Create components, apply BEM classesWrite documents' JavaScript
Save in development, if you agreeHave more permissions than the user it works as
Create translation drafts with Lingua BridgePublish or overwrite a translation that's already live

Every change goes through the same security filters as yours: an agent working as a user without permission to write code can't add scripts, event handlers or javascript: links. Every write through abilities ends up in Histeon's operations log.

The content the agent reads from the site is data, not an instruction. Its manual tells it so explicitly: if it finds “do this” written on a page, it doesn't do it and reports it to you.

What the agent may do on this site #

In the ✦ panel, What the agent may do, an administrator chooses the areas an agent may change: page texts, structure and attributes, selector CSS, components, media library, contents and fields, queries and JSON sources. Reading, undoing and saving to development are always allowed; publishing never is. The choice applies to the whole site, also to agents working from outside with the WordPress abilities.

  • Contents and fields is off until you turn it on: the title, excerpt and fields of a post or a record change the site at once. The status never changes (a draft stays a draft) and Undo all puts the previous values back.
  • Media: the agent searches files, uploads new ones through the builder's compression, writes alternative texts, sorts the collections and compresses an image already uploaded. It never deletes a file.
  • Queries and sources: written to development, with the usual versions.
  • Components: besides creating them, it changes their markup and props; every page that uses them follows.

While the agent changes something, the canvas has a coloured border and the label The agent is at work, with the area it is touching.

Keep or undo all #

When an AI agent changes the site through the abilities, Histeon remembers how each document was before its first change. Changes made without pauses longer than half an hour form a session. A bar shows in the builder: The AI agent is working (or has finished), how many documents it changed and which. Two buttons:

  • Keep: the changes stay in development, the bar goes away.
  • Undo all: every document goes back to how it was before the agent, as a new version in development. The agent's versions stay in the history: if you change your mind, restore them from Versions.

Either way nothing goes live: a release is always needed. An administrator also sees other users' sessions.

The Histeon CSS generator is not AI #

In the Design system panel, the Histeon CSS generator creates palettes, scales and shapes from a few values (base colors, minimum and maximum sizes, ratios). It doesn't use artificial intelligence and doesn't call external services: the values are calculated on your server, always the same way. It's explained in the chapter Histeon CSS.

The sections created with Create a section use exactly those tokens: when you change the design system, they change with it.

Good practices #

  • Ask the agent to propose what it will do before big changes.
  • Work in small steps and watch the canvas: undoing is always possible.
  • Before publishing, use Differences and Compare in the Publish window.
  • For abilities, create a dedicated user for the agent, with the minimum permissions it needs.
  • Keep the agent on localhost or on a development copy when you try new things.

Importing an Etch site

If your site was built with Etch, you don't have to rebuild it from scratch. Histeon reads the pages, components, templates, styles and queries saved with Etch and turns them into Histeon content. The result arrives as a development version: you check it, publish it when you're happy with it, and you can always go back.

Before you start #

A few things to know:

  • Etch's data is not touched. Etch's options, meta and content stay where they are; the import only writes to Histeon's development versions.
  • Nothing goes live by itself. The public site only changes when you publish a release.
  • The site works even without importing. If Etch isn't active, Histeon renders the content saved with Etch by itself, at request time, and the site stays as it was. Importing is still recommended, but it's never mandatory.
  • The import has been tested on a test site built with Etch 1.7.1 and Automatic.css: the imported pages turned out the same as the original, apart from the small differences described further on, with working scripts.

Back up the site before you start, as with any major change. The import is reversible, but a backup costs nothing.

The reminder #

As long as the site has content saved by Etch that hasn't been imported yet, administrators see the Histeon: import recommended notice on the admin screens, with two buttons:

  • Import now takes you to the import section;
  • Later hides the notice for 14 days, just for you.

The same notice also appears when Automatic.css isn't active and the stylesheets already on the site stay loaded (see further on).

Histeon's First run screen, too, at the “Where you're coming from” step, points out Etch content with the Prepare the import button.

Importing step by step #

  1. Open Histeon → Settings and go to the Import from Etch section. It appears only if Histeon finds Etch data on the site.
  2. If the site uses Automatic.css, choose where to take its design system (see below).
  3. Press Import preview. Histeon reads everything and shows you the report, without changing anything.
  4. Read the report, especially the To review column.
  5. Press Prepare in development. Now the import is written, but only in development.
  6. Open the pages in the builder and check them in preview.
  7. Publish from the builder with Publish…. In the publish window you'll find Differences and Compare to see what changes.

After publishing, the pages are rendered by Histeon.

The report #

The report summarizes how many documents, components, queries and bytes of CSS were found, then lists each document:

ColumnWhat it says
DocumentTitle, type and ID of the content
Trusttrusted if the author and the last person to edit it could write code, otherwise restricted
ConvertedHow many blocks were converted
ScriptHow many element scripts were carried over
To reviewWhat Histeon couldn't convert and needs fixing by hand

Below the table are the general warnings, for example the non-public content types that the imported queries use and that Histeon has allowed in loops.

Why trust matters. In Histeon each piece of content keeps the trust level of whoever wrote it, not of whoever imports it. JavaScript from authors without the unfiltered_html permission is not imported. That way dangerous content saved by an author doesn't become “trusted” just because an administrator imports it.

What gets converted #

From EtchIn Histeon
Pages, postsHisteon documents, with the same structure
ComponentsHisteon components, with a key derived from the name in Etch (EventCard becomes event-card)
Templates and template partsHisteon templates, with versions like everything else
Elements and textsHisteon elements, with tags, attributes and labels
Element scriptsThe document's JavaScript (only from trusted authors)
Loops and queriesPredefined loop queries, usable like the others
Dynamic imagesImages with dimensions and responsive versions read from the media library
Per-selector stylesHisteon per-selector styles
Global stylesheetsHisteon global stylesheets
Expressions in curly bracesHisteon expressions
WordPress and other plugins' blocksThey stay as they are, with the Etch blocks inside them converted

Classes added by scripts are protected, so CSS on demand doesn't discard them.

If Histeon doesn't have a CSS framework chosen yet and finds Automatic.css (or Core Framework), it picks that one. The import also turns on the Histeon CSS reset.

What doesn't get converted #

Histeon never guesses: what it doesn't recognize stays as it was and ends up in the To review column.

  • Etch conditions, slots and SVG blocks: they aren't supported yet and aren't converted. They have to be redone by hand in the builder.
  • Expressions that can't be translated: they stay as text, visible on the page, and appear in the report. Etch modifiers with an equivalent are translated, including the newest ones (toSlug, isTruthy, isFalsy, comparisons with values for true and false); those without one, such as round or toInt, are named in the report.
  • JavaScript from untrusted authors: it isn't imported (see above).
  • Fields added by other plugins to Etch's data (for example this.extra.field): they only work if the plugin also offers them to Histeon. It's one line of code for whoever maintains that plugin.
  • The CSS reset: it isn't copied. The import uses Histeon's reset, which leaves links placed directly in a container inline. If they were blocks on the original site and you need the same effect, add a rule to your CSS.

Check the imported pages in the preview: small differences may appear in texts, for example where the original showed an HTML entity encoded twice and Histeon shows it once.

The design system: Automatic.css #

If the site uses Automatic.css and its generated stylesheets are on the site, the Import from Etch section asks “The site uses Automatic.css: where should its design system go?”:

ChoiceWhat happens
Into Histeon CSS (recommended)Histeon's free framework, already included: its tokens take Automatic.css's values, the palette starts from your colors, equivalent classes are renamed, the rest keeps working. No external plugin
Into Core FrameworkAppears if Core Framework is installed: a project with the colors and all of Automatic.css's variables, plus its rules that the site uses
Stay on Automatic.cssNothing changes: the site keeps using Automatic.css

The choice reads the values from the generated stylesheets on the site.

From Automatic.css to Histeon CSS, without Etch #
