A site that doesn't use Etch can also move from Automatic.css to Histeon CSS: you do it from Histeon → Settings → Framework switch, with the same preview and the same “Prepare in development”. The full procedure, with the table of colors and names, is in the chapter Histeon CSS.

If Automatic.css isn't active. The stylesheets already in the site's uploads folder stay loaded until you import, and the site stays as it was, without running any Automatic.css code. Don't delete that folder before importing.

After publishing #

  • Check the public site, on a computer and on a phone.
  • Etch's data stays in the database: should you need to go back, restore the previous release in Histeon → Releases.
  • The “import recommended” notice stays as long as some content outside the trash still contains Etch blocks: check which pages you haven't published, or hide it with Later.

Exporting to Etch #

If a site must stay on Etch, you can take what you built with Histeon back to Etch: published pages, posts, components, templates, styles and loops go back into Etch's data on this site, with the same addresses and the same components. You do it from Histeon → Settings → Export to Etch, which appears when the site has Etch data or the Etch plugin.

  1. Choose the token names. Histeon CSS (recommended) adds two global stylesheets with the --xb-* tokens and the Histeon CSS classes, and the site stays editable through the tokens. Automatic.css names only appears if Automatic.css is already installed on the site: the tokens that have an ACSS name take it (--xb-space-m becomes --space-m).
  2. Press Export preview. Nothing changes: you see every document, how many blocks go through, how many are left out, whether it replaces content that was already Etch's, and which Etch entries (queries, stylesheets, styles) are added or replaced.
  3. If you like, press Download the JSON file to keep a copy of what would be written. Etch does not import this file.
  4. Tick the confirmation and press Write to this site. Histeon writes only what you saw in the preview; if something changed in the meantime, it stops and asks you to run the preview again. Before writing it makes a backup.
  5. If something is wrong, Restore puts content, component keys and Etch's options back as they were before the last write. Histeon's versions stay too: a release brings them back.

From WP-CLI: wp xb export-etch --user=<administrator> for the preview and the fingerprint, then --apply --confirm=<fingerprint> to write and --restore --yes to go back.

What is left out, and shown in the preview: conditions and slots (not supported yet), Histeon content types and records and the loops that list them, loops over terms or users, Histeon CMS templates, component prop schemas, and Histeon settings with no Etch equivalent. JavaScript from authors without the unfiltered_html permission is not exported, just as it is not imported. Writing needs an administrator with unfiltered_html.

For another Etch site: first bring the project to that site with Histeon installed (through the bridge or an .xbsite file), then export there. The final check belongs on a copy of the site with Etch active.

Modules and updates

Histeon is a single plugin, but some of its parts can be turned on and off: these are the modules. This chapter explains how to manage them, what happens if one fails, and how Histeon updates itself safely, without licenses.

What a module is #

A module is a part of Histeon that does a specific job and that not every site uses. Today there are three modules:

ModuleWhat it doesDefault
Histeon CSSHisteon's free CSS framework: fluid tokens, palettes, on-demand classes, recipes, the pop-up panel and the import from Automatic.cssOn
Static BridgeThe site as a static copy: export, publishing to Cloudflare, forms and languagesOff
Lingua BridgeTranslations as regular WordPress pages: editor on the site, memory, updates of changed sentences, AIOff

Static Bridge and Lingua Bridge also exist as separate plugins, with their own releases. Inside Histeon they're the same code, in the version included in the Histeon release.

Turning a module on and off #

  1. Open Histeon → Modules (you need to be an administrator).
  2. Each module has a card with an icon, status, description, version and requirements.
  3. Use the switch to turn it on or off. The Settings link takes you to its settings.

Turning off a module never deletes its data. Languages, translations, memory, static copies and settings stay: when you turn the module back on, you find them as they were.

What happens when you turn on a module:

  • Lingua Bridge does what activating the plugin would do: it prepares its tables and its address rules. Its menu appears under Histeon.
  • Static Bridge first checks that the server is suitable (PHP version, WordPress, memory). If something's wrong, nothing changes and the reasons appear on the screen. Its menu also appears under Histeon.
  • Histeon CSS, when off, leaves only the base tokens that components use; the classes, the panel and the palettes in Gutenberg disappear.

The statuses you may see:

StatusWhat it means
ActiveThe module is on and working
OffThe module is included but turned off
Active as a separate pluginThe same software is active as a plugin: the module stays idle
Requires PHP …The server has a PHP version that's too old (Static Bridge requires PHP 8.4)
Coming as a moduleNot included yet: today it's used as a separate plugin
Turned off after a fatal errorHisteon stopped it because it was failing (below)
Turned off by wp-config.phpWhoever manages the server turned it off from wp-config.php

Module or separate plugin #

The same code can't run twice: it would bring the site down. That's why:

  • if Lingua Bridge or Static Bridge are active as plugins, the module doesn't load and can't be turned on. The message says “It is active as a separate plugin: deactivate that to use the module”;
  • with the module on, on the Plugins page the separate plugin shows “Active as a Histeon module” instead of Activate.

To move from the plugin to the module: deactivate the plugin from the Plugins page, then turn on the module in Histeon → Modules. The data is the same (same options, same tables), so there's nothing to migrate.

A module is updated together with the Histeon release that contains it. The separate plugin, on the other hand, keeps getting its own updates.

When a module fails #

If a module causes a fatal error, Histeon stops it, and the rest of the site keeps working.

  1. The request in which the error happens is interrupted, as would happen with any plugin.
  2. From the next request on, the site starts again without that module.
  3. Every admin screen shows the notice Histeon turned off a module after a fatal error, with the module's name, the file, the line and the error message, and the Open Histeon → Modules button.
  4. On the Modules screen the status becomes Turned off after a fatal error.

What to do: update Histeon (or fix the cause, if you know it), then turn the module back on. Turning it back on clears the recorded error.

Only errors that originate inside a module turn it off. An error in Histeon itself, or in another plugin, doesn't turn off any module.

Safe mode #

If the admin no longer opens, someone with access to the site's files can turn off the modules from wp-config.php:

define( 'XB_SAFE_MODE', true );                               // nessun modulo
define( 'XB_DISABLE_MODULES', 'lingua-bridge,static-bridge' ); // solo questi

A module turned off this way can't be turned back on from the screen, which shows “Turned off by wp-config.php”. Removing the line returns the module to the state you had chosen.

Histeon updates #

Histeon updates itself from the releases published on GitHub, like any plugin, from the WordPress admin: no license, no telemetry. The site only asks which release is the latest and downloads its package and signature, without sending content, users or settings.

Updates are off by default. To turn them on:

  1. Open Histeon → Settings, Updates section.
  2. Check Check for updates on GitHub.
  3. In Repository, write the releases repository, in the form owner/name.
  4. In Minisign public key, paste the release public key (below).
  5. Press Save.

If you prefer, you can set them in wp-config.php with XB_UPDATE_REPOSITORY and XB_UPDATE_PUBLIC_KEY: in that case those values apply and the screen says so.

Histeon checks at most every 12 hours. When it finds a newer, signed version, WordPress offers it as a normal plugin update. Below the settings you see the latest signed release found and the installed version.

Updates only work with a public repository: a private repository doesn't respond to anonymous requests.

The copy before an update #

Before WordPress replaces Histeon with a new version (from the plugins page, with automatic updates, with WP-CLI or by uploading a ZIP), Histeon copies its own folder and its tables (versions, releases, log, deploys). If the copy fails, the update stops and says why.

In Histeon → Settings → Histeon safety copies you see the last two copies, can make one now and, after a confirmation, go back to the previous version. The copy does not include the site's content (pages, posts, media, menus), Histeon's settings, the modules' data, other plugins or the theme: the site backup covers those. On nginx add location ~ /xb-backups/ { deny all; } to the configuration.

Why releases are signed #

Every Histeon package is signed with a key that stays with whoever publishes the releases, outside the repository and the packages. Only the public key, used for verification, goes onto sites.

Before installing an update, Histeon downloads the package and its signature and checks two things:

  • that the signature matches the public key you configured;
  • that the signature names that very package.

If something doesn't add up, the update stops, the reason is shown and the downloaded file is deleted. That way, even if someone managed to slip a fake package among the releases, your site wouldn't install it.

Histeon's release public key, from version 0.1.0 on, is:

RWQSTenArP5FclHgI0qEErGDYUEQ6gL4cEhpgec49VKtqKfgl2TIAn5W

Verifying a release by hand #

If you install Histeon by uploading the ZIP by hand, you can verify it yourself with minisign, a small free program.

  1. From the release, download both the ZIP (xb-X.Y.Z-….zip) and its signature (the same name with .minisig at the end), into the same folder.
  2. Open a terminal in that folder and run, with the real file name:
minisign -Vm xb-X.Y.Z-sha.zip -P RWQSTenArP5FclHgI0qEErGDYUEQ6gL4cEhpgec49VKtqKfgl2TIAn5W
  1. If the package is intact and signed with the Histeon key, minisign replies that the signature and the trusted comment are valid, and shows the trusted comment: it must name the same file.
  2. If minisign reports an error, don't install that package.

Always compare the public key with the one written in this manual or in the official documentation, not with a key found next to the downloaded file.

Permissions and security

Different people work on a site: whoever builds, whoever publishes, whoever only writes the texts. Histeon uses WordPress roles and permissions to decide who can do what, and adds a few permissions of its own. This chapter explains them, and describes in plain words what Histeon does to keep the site safe.

Histeon permissions #

Histeon adds four permissions (in WordPress they're called capabilities):

PermissionWhat it allows
xb_buildUsing the builder: editing pages, styles, components and predefined queries, and saving in development
xb_publishPublishing releases and restoring earlier ones: that is, changing the live site
xb_manage_deployManaging the static site destinations (Cloudflare) and their credentials
xb_edit_contentClient mode: changes texts and images of the pages one may edit, in the builder, in development. It is the permission of the Histeon client role.
xb_edit_propsIn client mode, also changes the simple props of the components on those pages (text, number, yes/no, link, image, a choice from a list), never expressions or code. Off by default: no role receives it, not even Histeon client; you grant it, with a role editor or with wp cap add xb_client xb_edit_props.

When Histeon is activated, administrators receive all of them. Histeon also creates the Histeon client role: it can edit (not publish or delete) pages and posts, upload images and use the builder only in client mode. It changes pages made with Histeon only from the builder, never from the block editor. Other roles have no Histeon permissions until you give them.

Who can do what #

Besides the Histeon permissions, the normal WordPress ones count too:

To…You need
Open a page in the builderxb_build and permission to edit that page
Edit templates and template partsAlso permission to edit the theme (edit_theme_options)
Publish with Publish…xb_publish
Upload images and filesThe WordPress permission to upload files
Write JavaScript and free-form codeunfiltered_html (below)
Settings, Modules, local fontsBeing an administrator (with xb_build)
Export and import releases, move the siteBeing an administrator
Configure CloudflareBeing an administrator, with xb_manage_deploy

Without xb_publish you can build and save in development, but not put anything live: the Releases screen tells you so (“Publishing requires the xb_publish permission: ask whoever manages the site.”). It's a simple way to let a collaborator or an agency work while you keep the last word.

Anyone who only edits texts (for example a client) can keep using the WordPress block editor, which shows Histeon pages as they appear on the site.

What each client may do #

By default a client (the Histeon client role) changes texts and images of every page they may edit. In Histeon → Clients (or from the Histeon permissions link in the list of users) whoever builds or administers the site chooses, client by client:

  • what they may change: texts and images (always), the component props (like xb_edit_props; if the role already gives it, the box stays ticked) and the look of the page, with the groups of the Aspetto panel they may use (Layout, Spacing, Size, Text, Colours and borders, Position);
  • on which pages: every page they may edit, or only a list. In the list each page has a level: None (they do not open it, nor edit it in WordPress), View only, in the builder, Texts and pictures, Also the look. The pages you do not choose stay without access.

The look of the page applies there only. If the client changes the colour of a heading on the «Contacts» page, it changes on that page only: the same class on the other pages stays as it is. They do it from the Histeon · Aspetto panel in Gutenberg or from the Look of this page section in the builder, with the buttons of the groups you gave them only; they save in development and it goes live with the next release, like texts. You too, in Gutenberg, can write a style for the page only with the This page only switch.

No client gets new permissions with the update: until you choose something, what applied before still applies. The client does not see this screen and cannot change their own permissions or anyone else's: the server refuses every save beyond what you gave them.

Giving permissions to other roles #

Histeon has no roles screen of its own: it uses WordPress's. To give xb_build or xb_publish to a role (for example Editor), use any role and capability management plugin, look for the permissions starting with xb_ and assign them.

An example of how to split the work:

PersonWordPress roleHisteon permissions
Whoever manages the siteAdministratorAll
External developerEditorxb_build
Content managerEditorxb_build, xb_publish
Client who changes the textsAuthor or EditorNone: uses the block editor

Give everyone only what they need. This also applies to AI agents: they work with the permissions of the user they connect as, and a user without xb_publish can't publish anything. See Histeon and artificial intelligence.

Who can write code #

In WordPress, the unfiltered_html permission decides who can insert free-form code: scripts, event handlers, any tag. On a single site, administrators and editors usually have it; authors and contributors don't. Histeon respects it everywhere.

WhoLevel in HisteonWhat they can write
Has unfiltered_htmltrustedAny element and attribute, the JavaScript of pages and components
Doesn't have itrestrictedContent elements, static SVGs, styles; no scripts, no onclick and the like, no javascript: links

What you see in practice:

  • the JavaScript panel is read-only for anyone without unfiltered_html, with a message explaining why;
  • if a “restricted” person edits a page containing code written by a “trusted” person, that code stays intact and locked: they can neither change it nor duplicate it;
  • conversely, content written by a restricted person stays restricted even when an administrator saves, publishes or restores the page that contains it. Code never becomes “trusted” just because it passes through the right hands.

The rules apply on every route: the builder, the block editor, release and Etch site imports, AI agents.

You don't write PHP in the builder, by design: it would be code run on the server within reach of anyone who edits content. The site's PHP, in the theme's or a plugin's files, is used in the builder as data and dynamic blocks.

What Histeon does for security #

Here are the main protections, in plain words.

The builder is protected.

  • The builder page has a strict content security policy (CSP): only Histeon code runs, and the page can't be embedded in other sites.
  • The preview in the canvas is isolated: the page's scripts don't run, links don't navigate and forms aren't submitted.
  • The canvas loads images, video, fonts and stylesheets only from the site (its media CDN included) and from the origins written in Histeon → Settings → External origins allowed in the canvas: so the address of whoever is working never reaches third-party sites. An image from another origin shows as a grey rectangle with the origin in its tooltip; on the published page it stays as it is. Fonts of an external stylesheet (Google Fonts, for example) do not reach the canvas either: better to download them to the site (Design system → Site fonts).
  • The builder's requests to the site are checked against the user's permissions and WordPress's security codes.

Nothing goes live by mistake.

  • Everything you save goes to development. The site only changes with a release, and every release can be restored.
  • Every saved version has a fingerprint (SHA-256) that is checked again when it's read. A tampered release is not restored.
  • Histeon keeps an operations log of important events: publishes and restores, imports, exports, configuration, changes made by AI agents. Administrators read it in Histeon → Log, filtered by operation, person and days, and export it as CSV.

Previews and the static site don't let anything slip out.

  • Preview links are signed, they expire, they're valid only for that page and that user, and they aren't indexed. They can all be revoked at once.
  • Before publishing the static copy to Cloudflare or downloading it as a zip, Histeon checks it: if it finds private or session data (server paths, security codes, preview tokens), it stops.

Credentials and personal data.

  • The Cloudflare token goes in wp-config.php or is saved encrypted, and it's never shown or returned.
  • Form messages are visible only to administrators, are kept for 180 days and can be exported or erased with the WordPress privacy tools.
  • Fonts can be hosted on the site, so pages don't ask Google's servers for the fonts.

Signed updates, no license.

  • No license key and no telemetry.
  • Updates are off until you turn them on, and they're installed only if signed with the Histeon key. See Modules and updates.

No external calls you didn't ask for. Histeon connects to external services only for the features you turn on:

FeatureWhere toWhen
UpdatesGitHubOnly if you turn them on, at most every 12 hours
Local fontsGoogle FontsOnly when you import a font, once
Static siteCloudflareOnly if you configure publishing
Machine translation (Lingua Bridge)The service you chooseOnly with your own key, on request
YouTube or Vimeo videoYouTube or VimeoWhen you insert a video: title and preview image, once
JSON source From an addressThe address you writeOnly if you create a source of this kind
Sending from staging to productionThe paired production siteOnly when you send
Names suggested by a model in Convert an HTML pageAnthropic, or a compatible server you chooseOnly if you set your own key or server

AI agents on a leash. The connector accepts only a closed list of operations, listens only on your computer and can't publish. WordPress abilities work with the user's permissions, and an agent doesn't publish on its own: the request to publish leads to the Releases page, where a person publishes.

On uninstall, Histeon removes its tables, options, secrets and permissions. The pages' content stays.

Hardened installation #

Every WordPress plugin, Histeon's modules included, runs in the same PHP process with the same rights on the database and the files: a weak spot in any of them concerns the whole site. So an effective way to reduce the risk is not to split plugins apart, but to take WordPress out of the visitors' way. When the site allows it, this setup is the one to choose.

  1. Static public site. Publish the static copy to Cloudflare or serve it with Static Bridge (see The static site). Visitors get only HTML and files: they run no PHP and never touch the database. Only loop filters and forms stay dynamic, if you use them.
  2. Private administration. Make wp-admin and wp-login.php reachable only by the people who work on the site: a server rule for trusted IP addresses, a VPN, or an access service such as Cloudflare Access in front of WordPress's address. With publishing to Cloudflare, WordPress can live at an address the public doesn't know.
  3. Only the modules you use. Turn off in Histeon → Modules the ones you don't need: a module that is off registers no pages, routes or tasks. To be sure even if someone flips the switch back, write it in wp-config.php:
define( 'XB_DISABLE_MODULES', 'lingua-bridge,static-bridge' );
  1. Credentials out of the database. Put the Cloudflare token in wp-config.php (XB_CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN) rather than in the settings.
  2. No file editing from wp-admin. With define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_EDIT', true ); in wp-config.php nobody can edit theme and plugin code from the administration.
  3. Few users with strong permissions. Give unfiltered_html and xb_publish only to those who need them (see Who can write code).
  4. Signed updates. Turn them on and let Histeon install only signed versions (see Modules and updates).

You need neither Docker nor a dedicated server: all of this works on ordinary hosting too, with the static site on Cloudflare. Self-hosters can go one step further and keep WordPress on an internal network.

The interface in English and Italian

Histeon is written in Italian and is also available in English. This chapter explains how it chooses the language it speaks to you in, and what is left out of that choice.

This is about the language of the interface: menus, buttons, messages. To publish the site in several languages you need Lingua Bridge: see Multilingual sites.

How the language is chosen #

Histeon uses the same language WordPress uses for you:

  1. the language of your profile (Users → Profile → Language);
  2. if the profile doesn't set one, the language of the site (Settings → General → Site Language).

Then the rule is simple:

Your language in WordPressHisteon speaks to you in
ItalianItalian
EnglishEnglish
Any other (German, French, Spanish…)English

So anyone using WordPress in German or French reads English, not Italian: it's the language they're most likely to understand. If the site has a Histeon translation for your own language, Histeon uses that.

Changing the language #

Each person can have their own language, on the same site:

  1. Open Users → Profile.
  2. Under Language, choose the one you prefer.
  3. Save the profile and reload the builder.

That way a collaborator can work in English while you carry on in Italian, or the other way around. The choice doesn't change anything on the public site.

Where it applies #

The same rule applies to every part of Histeon, each with its own translations:

  • Histeon: the admin screens, the builder and the Histeon panels in the block editor;
  • Histeon CSS, the framework and its panel;
  • Lingua Bridge, the languages module;
  • Static Bridge, the static site module;
  • Fondamenta, the base theme, and XB Blank, the compatibility theme.

This holds both when you use them as Histeon modules and as separate plugins.

What doesn't change with the language #

Some things stay the same in every language, because they aren't interface texts:

  • Technical names: CSS classes, variables, permission names (xb_build, xb_publish), the syntax of expressions and code.
  • Product names: Histeon, Histeon CSS, Lingua Bridge, Static Bridge.
  • Instructions for AI agents: the manual and the instructions the connector hands to the agent stay in their original form.
  • The site's content: the texts of your pages, and also the ready-made texts of the Fondamenta and XB Blank themes (such as “Pagina non trovata”, Italian for “Page not found”), are content, not interface. Whoever manages the site translates them, with Lingua Bridge too.

The words in Italian #

If you work with colleagues who use the interface in Italian, here's what the main things are called:

EnglishItalian
development, in developmentsviluppo, in sviluppo
liveonline, produzione
publish, releasepubblicare, release
draftbozza
componentcomponente
canvastela
stylesheetfoglio di stile
selector, classselettore, classe
global stylesstili globali
content type, fieldtipo di contenuto, campo
data sourcesorgente dati
named media querymedia query con nome
loop queryquery dei loop
previewanteprima
restoreripristina
Undo, RedoAnnulla, Ripeti
checkscontrolli
site movetrasloco del sito
static site, static copysito statico, copia statica
translation memorymemoria di traduzione
language switcherselettore di lingua

Keyboard shortcuts don't change with the interface language: Ctrl+K opens the commands in both English and Italian. See Keyboard shortcuts.

Keyboard shortcuts

In the builder almost everything can also be done from the keyboard. This chapter lists the default shortcuts, the keys that work in particular places (the canvas, the structure, the code) and explains how to change them.

On a Mac, use ⌘ instead of Ctrl and ⌥ instead of Alt. The tables give the Windows and Linux version.

The command palette #

The most useful shortcut is Ctrl+K: it opens the command palette, from which you can reach the whole builder. Type a few letters, choose with ↑ and ↓, confirm with Enter. Esc closes it. The Commands button in the top bar opens it too.

Next to each command the palette shows its shortcut, if it has one. That way you learn them while you work.

Default shortcuts #

CommandWindows and LinuxMac
Open commandsCtrl+K⌘+K
Save in developmentCtrl+S⌘+S
Add an elementCtrl+I⌘+I
UndoCtrl+Z⌘+Z
RedoCtrl+Shift+Z or Ctrl+Y⌘+Shift+Z or ⌘+Y
Duplicate the selected elementCtrl+D⌘+D
Delete the selected elementDeleteDelete
Move upAlt+↑⌥+↑
Move downAlt+↓⌥+↓
Move out of the containerAlt+←⌥+←
Move into the element beforeAlt+→⌥+→
Cut the selected elementCtrl+X⌘+X

Esc is fixed and can't be changed: it closes only the open popover (History, More, AI connector), otherwise the open section, then the framework panel, then clears the selection.

While you type, shortcuts don't steal your keys. In text fields and code editors only “Open commands” and “Save in development” work. “Add an element” also works in fields, but not in code. All the others work only outside fields and editors.

Shortcuts also work after a click inside the canvas: the preview passes the keys on to the builder.

Copying and pasting elements #

Outside text fields and code:

KeysWhat it does
Ctrl+CCopies the selected element with the styles of its classes
Ctrl+VPastes: an element copied from Histeon (even from another site) opens the paste window; HTML copied from elsewhere is cleaned up and inserted as elements

Keys in particular places #

These keys work only in one specific place in the builder and can't be changed.

Text typed in the canvas (double-click on a heading, a paragraph, a link or a button):

KeysWhat it does
EnterConfirms the text (so does a click outside)
Shift+EnterAdds a line break within the same element
EscPuts the text back as it was
Ctrl+B / Ctrl+IBold / italic
Ctrl+SConfirms and saves in development

Structure panel:

KeysWhat it does
F2Renames the element (so does a double-click on the name)
Shift+F10 or the Menu keyOpens the element's context menu

Context menu, palette and Add menu: ↑ and ↓ to move (in the Add menu also ← and →), Enter to choose, Esc to close. In the context menu Home and End go to the first and last item.

HTML and code panel:

KeysWhat it does
TabExpands an Emmet abbreviation (for example ul>li*3), or indents the line
Esc, then TabLeaves the code editor with the keyboard (the hint appears at the bottom right while the editor has the focus)
F12 or Ctrl+click on a class or an idSelects the element and opens the CSS panel on that selector

Canvas width handles (on the sides of the preview, after reaching them with Tab):

KeysWhat it does
← / →Narrows or widens by 10 pixels
Shift+← / Shift+→Narrows or widens by 100 pixels
HomeMinimum width
End or EscBack to full width (so does a double-click on the handle)

Preview: Esc goes back to the builder.

Lingua Bridge's editor on the site: Ctrl+Enter saves the text's translation and moves to the next one.

Changing shortcuts #

Every palette command can have up to three combinations: actions, sections, panels, snippets, components. Only the “Select” entries are left out, since they change from one page to another.

  1. Open the palette with Ctrl+K and choose Keyboard shortcuts….
  2. Search for the command by name, by group or by combination.
  3. Press + next to the command. “Press the combination… (Esc cancels)” appears.
  4. Press the combination you want. It's saved immediately.

To remove a combination, press ✕ next to it. To go back to a command's default shortcuts, press ↺; to go back to the defaults for the whole builder, Restore all.

Your shortcuts are saved in your profile: they apply to you, on every browser and computer, and don't change your colleagues' shortcuts.

Rules for combinations #

  • Form: Ctrl, Alt and Shift (in this order, whichever you want) plus one key. The key can be a letter, a digit, F1 to F12, an arrow, Delete, Home, End, Page Up, Page Down or one of , . / ; [ ] = -.
  • One combination, one command. If it's already in use, Histeon tells you, with the name of the command that uses it (“… already belongs to …”): remove that one first.
  • Reserved combinations, which stay with the browser or with typing and can't be assigned: Ctrl+C, Ctrl+V, Ctrl+X, Ctrl+A, Ctrl+W, Ctrl+T, Ctrl+N, Ctrl+Q, Ctrl+R, Ctrl+L, Ctrl+F, Ctrl+P, Ctrl+Shift+T, Ctrl+Shift+N, Ctrl+Shift+W and Backspace.
  • The physical key. Letters, digits and punctuation are recognized by the key's position, not by the character it produces: that's why Alt (or ⌥ on a Mac) doesn't change them.

With a keyboard whose layout is very different (for example AZERTY), if a shortcut doesn't respond as you expect, record it again by pressing it: Histeon saves the key you actually press.

A shortcut runs the command exactly as the palette does, with the same checks: it doesn't allow anything the command doesn't already allow.

Troubleshooting and FAQ

The situations that come up most often, with the cause and what to do. If a problem isn't here, look at the chapter on that topic: the answer is often in the notes.

The builder and publishing #

The builder doesn't open, or I don't see “Edit with Histeon” #

The message “You don't have permission to edit this content with Histeon.” appears, or the link is missing from the list of pages.

To open a page in the builder you need two things: Histeon's xb_build permission and WordPress's permission to edit that page. By default only administrators have xb_build. For templates you also need permission to edit the theme.

What to do: ask whoever administers the site to give you xb_build (see Permissions and security). The builder opens on pages, posts, components and templates: not on other content types.

I saved, but nothing changes on the site #

That's the normal behavior. Save (or Ctrl+S) puts the changes in development: you see them in the builder and in Preview, but site visitors still see the live version. To put them live, use Publish… in the top bar.

If the problem persists after publishing, check:

  • the page in WordPress: if it's still a draft, visitors don't see it;
  • translated components: a component's translation is used only when it's published (see Multilingual sites);
  • the static copy: if the site is served as a static copy, see further on;
  • the browser or site cache: try reloading the page with a forced refresh.

I don't see the “Publish…” button #

The button appears only for those with the xb_publish permission. Without it, you can build and save in development, and someone else publishes: the Releases screen reminds you of this.

If the button is there but disabled, there are no changes to publish. If the Publish button stays grayed out in the publish window, the page checks found errors: fix them, then try again.

The HTML panel is read-only #

This happens when an element contains content written outside Histeon (for example in the block editor or by an import) that Histeon code can't represent faithfully. It's a protection: editing it could save something different from what you see. The message in the panel explains this. Fix the element from the structure or from the other panels.

The JavaScript panel is read-only #

Only users with the WordPress unfiltered_html permission can write JavaScript. This is by design: see Who can write code.

I published the wrong version #

Open Histeon → Releases, find the right release in the Release history and press Restore. The restored release goes live again as a new release, and the changes you have in development stay where they are. See Versions and releases.

The static site #

The static copy is out of date #

The static copy is a snapshot of the site: it changes only when it's regenerated (see Static site).

  • With Static Bridge: in Histeon → Releases, Static site section, check the status. If it says “updates itself on every Histeon release”, after a publish Static Bridge updates the pages involved within a short time (you see them as “pages queued for regeneration”). If it says “automatic updating turned off: regenerate from Static Bridge”, regenerate by hand from Open Static Bridge.
  • With Cloudflare: if automatic publishing is on, every release schedules a deploy in the background, which starts shortly after with WordPress's scheduled tasks. Otherwise, in the builder open the Versions section and press Publish to Cloudflare now.
  • Translated strings: a Lingua Bridge string applies to all the pages of a language. After changing one, regenerate the complete static copy.

The deploy or the zip stops with a private content error #

Before publishing to Cloudflare or creating the zip, Histeon checks every page. If it finds data that must not leave the site (server paths, security codes, preview links), it stops. The cause is usually a plugin that prints session data into pages even for anonymous visitors. Find the page indicated and fix the cause: the check can't be skipped.

Static Bridge won't turn on #

Static Bridge requires PHP 8.4. With an older PHP, the Modules screen shows “Requires PHP 8.4”. If PHP is fine, on the other hand, the reasons for the refusal appear on the same screen (memory, file functions, WordPress version).

Modules and updates #

A module turned itself off #

The notice “Histeon turned off a module after a fatal error” appears. Histeon stopped the module because it was causing an error, and the rest of the site works. Update Histeon (or fix the cause indicated in the notice), then turn the module back on in Histeon → Modules. See When a module fails.

The admin no longer opens #

If you suspect a module, someone with access to the site's files can add this line to wp-config.php:

define( 'XB_SAFE_MODE', true );

No module will be loaded. When the problem is solved, remove the line.

I can't turn on Lingua Bridge (or Static Bridge) #

If the message says “It is active as a separate plugin”, the same software is already active as a plugin: that's fine as it is, or deactivate the plugin and turn on the module. The data is the same.

If the screen says “Turned off by wp-config.php”, someone turned it off with XB_SAFE_MODE or XB_DISABLE_MODULES: that line has to be removed.

Histeon doesn't find updates #

Below the update settings, “No signed release found (or GitHub unreachable)” appears. Check that the repository is spelled correctly and that it's public: a private repository doesn't respond. Histeon tries again by itself every 12 hours.

An update was rejected #

Histeon installs only packages signed with the configured public key. If the signature doesn't match, the update stops and the downloaded file is deleted. Check that the key in the settings is Histeon's (you'll find it in Modules and updates); if it is, do not force the installation by hand.

Imports #

Importing a release or a site is rejected #

The message says, for example, “Invalid release file: content altered or incomplete (hash)”, or “Invalid site file: …”.

Every file exported by Histeon contains a fingerprint of its content. If the file was changed after the export, even by a comma, or the download was interrupted, the fingerprint doesn't match and Histeon rejects the whole file. Export it again from the source site and don't open or edit it before importing.

Other reasons for rejection: the file exceeds the size limits, or it exceeds the server's upload limit (“File not received (it may exceed the server's upload limit).”).

Content is missing after the site move #

If the source site has content types created in Histeon, on the first pass their content is skipped with a warning: WordPress recognizes them only after the settings are published. Publish the release, then import the same file again: the second import doesn't duplicate anything.

A translated XLIFF file won't import #

The original changed after the export. Export again: the translation tool will find the sentences already translated in its memory.

I imported an Etch site and some JavaScript is missing #

Histeon imports JavaScript only from content written by people who had permission to write code (unfiltered_html). The import report shows this in the Trust column. If the script is really needed, someone with that permission rewrites it in the builder. See Importing an Etch site.

I turned off Etch: will the site change? #

No. With Etch turned off, Histeon renders the content saved with Etch by itself and the site stays as it was. Importing is still recommended, because it makes everything editable in the builder.

I turned off (or removed) Automatic.css #

As long as the stylesheets Automatic.css generated stay in the site's uploads folder, they stay loaded until you import: classes and variables keep working. A notice reminds you that importing into Histeon CSS is recommended. Don't delete those stylesheets before importing: without them the pages would lose their styles.

Languages and AI agents #

The language switcher doesn't appear #

It appears only when the page has at least two published versions. Translations in draft don't count.

The translation made by the AI isn't live #

This is by design: agents, machine translation and XLIFF create drafts only, never published pages. Review the draft and publish it yourself.

The AI connector won't connect #

Check, in order:

  1. that the computer has Node 18 or later;
  2. that the builder is open over HTTPS or on localhost;
  3. that the connector was started with the command copied after the last regeneration of the secret: if you regenerated the secret, copy the instructions again and restart the connector;
  4. that you pressed Connect this tab in the ✦ panel.

Reloading the tab reopens the connection by itself; closing it closes the connection.

Frequently asked questions #

Do I need a license? No. Histeon is free (GPL), with no license keys. So are Histeon CSS, Lingua Bridge and Static Bridge.

Does Histeon send data to anyone? Not on its own: no telemetry. It connects to external services only for the features you turn on (for example updates, font imports, Cloudflare, machine translation with your own key), and an AI agent you connect receives what it reads from the site. See Permissions and security.

Can an AI agent break the live site? Not from the connector: it works only in development and can't publish. Not with WordPress abilities either: it can ask to publish, but a person publishes, from the Releases page.

Can I use Histeon in English? Yes: choose English in your WordPress profile. See The interface in English and Italian.

Can I change the shortcuts? Yes, for every palette command: Ctrl+K → Keyboard shortcuts…. See Keyboard shortcuts.

Does turning off a module delete data? No, never.
